The implementation must build/read the mesh **through the uniform mesh API only** (see the "Uniform mesh API rule" above) so it works under every mesh backend. **This step is optional** — a format can stay Python-only indefinitely (precedent: `neuroglancer` and the Simvia-contributed `ansysInp`/`openfoam`); don't force a C++ port just to satisfy this checklist.
3. Import the module in `src/python/meshioplusplus/__init__.py` (both the import tuple and `__all__`).
4. Add `tests/python/test_<format>.py` using `helpers.write_read()`. Verify a direct C++ roundtrip and cross-compat with the Python reference (if a C++ path exists).
5. **Update `README.md`, `doc/formats.md`, and this file** (see "Keeping docs in sync").

Do not copy test data from GPL sources (e.g. FEconv examples) into this MIT repo — generate reference files via round-trip instead. Code/fixtures from other MIT-licensed forks in the same lineage (e.g. the [meshlane](https://github.com/simvia-tech/meshlane) fork) may be copied directly; credit the source in `CITATION.cff`/`CHANGELOG.md`.

**Note on `gmsh`:** **`$Entities` is the only place format 4.1 records physical-group membership** — an `$Elements` block names an `(entityDim, entityTag)` pair and the physical tag hangs off the *entity*, unlike 2.2 where every element carries its own tag. Until v9.7.0 `read_gmsh` threw `Gmsh $Entities not supported by the C++ reader` on sight of it, and since `$Entities` is the **first** section of every file Gmsh 4.1 writes, **no real 4.1 file was readable** from WASM/C API/Fortran/Julia/R/the native CLI. Python never saw it: the shim's blanket `except Exception` silently re-read with the pure-Python reference reader, so the gap was invisible from the one surface that had a fallback — which is exactly why it survived so long, and the general lesson is that a shim that swallows every exception hides a core gap from the only tests most people run. v9.7.0 implements it in both directions. Three facts worth not rediscovering: the per-entity bounding box is **3 doubles for dim 0 and 6 otherwise** (the one dimension-dependent field in the layout; 4.0 uses 6 unconditionally, which is why 4.0 still declines); the size_t width is the header's `data_size` and real files use **both 4 and 8** (`example/example.msh` is a `4.1 0 4` file), so assuming 8 silently misreads every count; and bounding-entity tags are **signed** — the sign is the boundary's orientation — so they can never be a `Region` (whose entries are sorted non-negative indices) and instead ride a **`GmshInfo`** side channel (the `MedInfo`/`ExodusInfo` pattern: `read_gmsh(path, GmshInfo&, opts)` / `write_gmsh41(path, mesh, binary, const GmshInfo&)`, dropped by the registry and therefore by the flat bindings), surfacing in Python as `cell_sets["gmsh:bounding_entities"]` via the `_extra_cell_sets` passthrough. Adding those overloads makes `&read_gmsh`/`&write_gmsh41` **ambiguous as bare function pointers**, so `registry.cpp` wraps both in lambdas (the exodus/mdpa precedent) — a compile error, never silent. Two deliberate divergences from the Python reference: a file tagging only *some* entities gets one `gmsh:physical` array **per cell block** with `0` on the untagged ones, because `_gmsh41.py:255`'s `if physical_tag:` omits them and leaves the array shorter than `mesh.cells`, a shape the uniform mesh API cannot hold and `gmsh_attach_regions` rejects (a file tagging *nothing*, like `example/example.msh`, still gets no `gmsh:physical` at all — synthesizing all-zeros would invent a group); and the writer's entity set is the **union** of node entities and cell entities rather than node entities alone, because a straight curve whose only nodes are its endpoints owns none of its own yet still carries elements (`example/example.msh` has six such curves) and the Python writer drops their tags. `gmsh_entity_blocks_41` also accepts `gmsh:dim_tags` as a flat `2N` row as well as `(N,2)`, since the flat bindings carry data arrays without a shape — without that, WASM would silently emit no `$Entities`. `read_gmsh_metadata` parses `$Entities` + `$PhysicalNames` too (both small, both ahead of `$Elements`) so a 4.1 summary reports `gmsh:physical` and the named regions, with entry counts, from block headers alone. **`$Periodic` remains C++-unsupported in both directions** (still throws → Python fallback → unreadable on the flat bindings), and 4.0 still declines. `write_gmsh22` (2.2, attaches each element's physical tag directly in `$Elements`) round-trips named `Cell` region **membership** for any mesh; the registry-default `write_gmsh41` now does too, but only for a mesh carrying `point_data["gmsh:dim_tags"]` (any mesh read from a 4.1 file) — a mesh from another format has no entity structure to describe, so no `$Entities` is emitted and only the group **name** survives, and `gmsh22` stays the answer there. Its ASCII output is byte-identical to the Python reference writer's, pinned by a test. Both were always reachable from Python (`gmsh22_write`/`gmsh41_write`, `register_format`'s `"gmsh22"`/`"gmsh"` writer keys) but only `"gmsh"` → `write_gmsh41` was ever registered in the **shared** dispatch tables (`registry.cpp`), so WASM/C API/Fortran had no way to select the round-trip-capable writer at all. v8.7.0 adds a **`"gmsh22"` write-only key** to `registry_writers()` (no matching reader key — reading auto-detects the file's own `$MeshFormat` version and needs no separate entry); `write_gmsh22` already synthesized `gmsh:physical` from `Cell` regions when the mesh carried none of its own (`gmsh_tags_from_regions`), so no core logic changed, only its reachability. `availableFormats().writers` now includes `"gmsh22"` (write-only, like `"svg"`); `--output-format gmsh22` / `writeMesh(path, mesh, "gmsh22")` selects it on every surface `resolve_format`/`registry_writers()` reaches.

**Note on `ensight`:** EnSight Gold, geometry only (`.case` + `.geo` sibling pair; write logic ported from Kratos's `EnSightOutput`, reader is meshio++-original). ASCII + C-binary both ways; the binary reader auto-detects a foreign byte order from record plausibility. Per the Gold spec connectivity is **positional** (1-based into the part's coordinates) — `given`/`ignore` id arrays are skipped, matching VTK's readers (not Kratos's id-based writing assumption). Node ordering: meshio (VTK) order matches EnSight for every type except `wedge15` ↔ `penta15`, which differ by the involution `{0,2,1,3,5,4, 8,7,6, 11,10,9, 12,14,13}` — do **not** port Kratos's penta15/hexa20 group swaps (those fix Kratos-specific ordering). Multi-part files → concatenated points, one block per part-section, `cell_data["ensight:part"]` when ≥2 parts; the writer emits a single `assign`-id part and drops mesh data (warns). Ragged write raises (v1).

**Note on `vti`:** VTK XML ImageData (v9.25.0) -- a regular lattice whose geometry is the `Origin`/`Spacing`/`WholeExtent` attributes rather than a point array, so there is **no `<Points>` section at all**. `read_vti` expands the extent into explicit points and one `hexahedron` block through `detail/grid_lattice.hpp` (the third consumer of that header, and the one it was written anticipating), so `read_vti(write_vti(m)) == m` is an identity rather than a coincidence. `write_vti` therefore **requires a lattice**, via `detail::lattice_from_mesh`, and refuses anything else by name -- including a *partial* grid, whose holes ImageData cannot express and which would otherwise be silently filled in. Reader and writer live in **one** file (unlike VTU's split), since the geometry is three attributes; helpers are prefixed `vti_` (`vtu_` is taken). It reuses `detail/vtk_xml.hpp`'s `<DataArray>` codec and `detail/vtu_binary.hpp`'s base64 + 32 KiB block framing verbatim, so `--codec zlib|lz4|zstd` works here exactly as for vtu/vtp (three guard strings updated: both CLIs, `mcp/_tools.py`, `write_options.cpp`, the C header). It declines the same constructs VTU does (`<AppendedData>`, multiple `<Piece>`s, lzma) plus two of its own: a `Piece` whose `Extent` is not the `WholeExtent` (its arrays are sized to the *piece*, so reading them against the whole extent is silently misaligned) and a non-identity `Direction` (a rotated lattice, which an axis-aligned hexahedron grid cannot hold without baking the rotation into the coordinates). Two facts worth not rediscovering: `WholeExtent` counts **points**, so `0 16` is 16 cells and 17 planes; and an extent that does not start at zero puts the lo corner at `Origin + start*Spacing`, so dropping the offset translates the whole grid by a cell, silently and plausibly. The geometry attributes are written `%.17g` -- the stream default of six significant digits loses ~10 digits of a real origin and places the grid 1e-7 off its own points, which nothing downstream flags; the numpy twin uses the identical spelling so the two writers' attribute lines agree character for character. **The round trip through our own writer is a WEAK oracle here** (reader and writer share a convention, so a consistently wrong `Origin` survives it), which is why the tests also assert on the raw written bytes and read hand-written files whose expected geometry is stated independently. `read_vti_metadata` is the cheapest metadata path in the repo -- it decodes **nothing**, since the extent gives both counts, and it is the only one that can report a bounding box.

**Note on `vtp`:** VTK XML PolyData; shares the VTU stack — the `<DataArray>` helpers live in `detail/vtk_xml.hpp` (used by `vtu.cpp`/`vtu_read.cpp`/`vtp.cpp`/`vtp_read.cpp`; keep VTU byte-identical when touching it). Surface cells only; cell data follows VTK's canonical Verts→Lines→Polys order (writer reorders blocks into that partition). PolyData has no cell-type array, so 3-/4-noded polygons read back as triangle/quad. The pybind `vtp_write` binding opts into `allow_ragged` (jagged polygons are legal Polys rows).

**Note on `triangle`:** 2D analogue of tetgen (`.node`/`.ele` pairs → `triangle`/`triangle6`, `.poly` PSLG → `line` segments; holes/regions skipped with a warning). Extension dispatch: `.node`/`.ele` stay tetgen-first everywhere; the Python reader dispatcher falls through to triangle when tetgen raises on a 2D file, writes need `file_format="triangle"`; only `.poly` defaults to triangle (also in the C++ registry, so the flat bindings need an explicit format for 2D `.node`/`.ele`). The multi-file buffer guards in `_helpers.py` cover `tetgen`/`triangle`/`ensight`.

**Note on `exodus`:** the C++ reader used to `throw ReadError("Exodus: <key> handled by Python fallback")` on `qa_records`, `info_records`, `ns_names` and `node_ns*`. Since **every** file SEACAS/Cubit/Sierra writes carries `qa_records`, that made the format unreadable wherever there is no Python to defer to — i.e. all of WASM — while being invisible from Python, where the shim's blanket `except Exception: pass` silently swapped in the fallback. All four are now handled (v8.6.0). `qa_records`/`info_records` are strings and `NDArray` has **no string dtype**, so they cannot ride on the mesh: they travel in an `ExodusInfo` side-channel struct (`MedInfo`/`OpenFoamInfo`'s pattern) that the pybind binding `setattr`s as `mesh.info`, preserving the Python reference's output byte-for-byte; the flat bindings construct one and drop it, exactly as `registry.cpp` already does for `MedInfo` — a documented gap, not a silent loss. The **fixture is hand-authored** (`tests/python/exodus_fixture.py`, built at test time with `netCDF4` rather than committed as LFS) because meshio++'s own writer emits no `qa_records`, no `eb_names` and no side sets, so a round-trip test could not have caught any of this — the general lesson being that a format's own writer is not a sufficient test oracle for that format's reader. **v9.3.0 is the same lesson again, from the other end of the string.** A netCDF text attribute carries an explicit length, and NetCDF.jl (what PeriLab and other Julia solvers write Exodus with) counts the C string's terminating NUL as part of it, so a `SPHERE` block's `elem_type` arrives as 7 characters and matched no key in the C++ type table — `Exodus: unknown element type SPHERE`, with the NUL **invisible in the message** because `std::runtime_error::what()` is a `const char*` that stops at it, which is what made the diagnosis non-obvious. `netCDF4` strips the NUL on the way in, so the Python twin was fine and the shim's fallback hid it everywhere except WASM (VSCode-MDPA-Preview#63). Both readers now trim trailing NUL/space padding (`read_att_text` / `_elem_type`); `SPHERE → vertex` was already in both type tables and did not change. The real file is committed at `tests/python/meshes/exodus/DCBmodel_PD_solid.e` (Git-LFS, **BSD-3-Clause** from PeriLab — permissive, but its notice + licence text must travel with it, hence the `.license` sidecar and `LICENSE.BSD-3-Clause` beside it; credited in `CITATION.cff`), with `test_the_reference_file_still_carries_a_nul_terminated_elem_type` asserting the *encoding* by raw bytes — without it, a re-fetch from an upstream that switched netCDF writers would leave the whole regression suite passing while testing nothing. Note `wasm.yml` checks out **without** `lfs: true` (only `ci.yml` sets it), so `tests/wasm/smoke.mjs` must keep writing its own Exodus file rather than reaching for that fixture. A synthetic fixture cannot get the property naturally: `netCDF4` strips the NUL whatever spelling you pass, so `write_sphere_fixture` **patches the classic-format bytes**: an attribute is `[nc_type][nelems][value padded to 4]`, and `len("SPHERE") % 4 != 0` means 6 and 7 characters pad to the same size, so bumping the recorded count rewrites four bytes in place and shifts no offset; the gtest twin builds its file through the netCDF C API, where the length is just a parameter. The same release added **per-element attributes** (`attrib{k}`/`attrib_name{k}` — where a `SPHERE`'s radius, a beam's cross-section or a shell's thickness lives) in both directions as `cell_data` under `kExodusAttributePrefix` (`"exodus:attr:"`, twinned as `_exodus.ATTRIBUTE_PREFIX`). The prefix is load-bearing at **both** ends: on read it separates an attribute from a same-named `name_elem_var` element variable (constant-in-time vs per-step, different concepts), and on write it is the only signal marking which `cell_data` belongs in `attrib{k}` — do not "simplify" it into writing every cell_data as an attribute. Values are always Float64 and a block the file omits reads as **NaN**, which is not padding but the round-trip signal: the writer skips an all-non-finite block, so a mixed file comes back byte-equal instead of gaining NaN attributes. **v9.9.0 completed the writer**, which had been emitting only geometry, `point_data` and attributes: ordinary `cell_data` now goes out as **element variables** (`name_elem_var` + an all-ones `elem_var_tab` truth table + one `vals_elem_var{j}eb{k}` per variable x block, trailing dims becoming extra netCDF dimensions exactly as the nodal path already does, so a vector cell field survives); **`eb_names` is recovered from `Cell` regions** by inverting the read path (a region whose *canonical* entries are exactly the contiguous global range `[bases[k], bases[k+1])` is block `k`'s name -- a first/last check, since `AddRegion` sorts and de-duplicates -- written only when some block is actually named, so a region-less mesh's bytes are unchanged); and `time_whole` comes from **`field_data["exodus:time"]`** with both readers setting that key to the step they returned. The `exodus:attr:` prefix stays explicit for exactly the reason it always did: an attribute is a *different* Exodus concept, so "write every cell_data as an attribute" was never the rule. Element blocks therefore round-trip regions now; node sets and side sets still do not, so exodus stays partly in `READ_ONLY_REGIONS`. **The same work surfaced a heap buffer overflow in the reader**: assembling a cell_data array allocated a scalar `{total}` buffer and `memcpy`'d each block's full `Nbytes()` into it, writing `n*k` bytes into `n` bytes' worth of space -- pre-existing, unreachable until the writer started emitting element variables, and unreachable from any real SEACAS file (none carries a multi-component element variable), which is the same "a format's own writer is not a sufficient test oracle for its reader" lesson twice over. A multi-step Exodus *writer* remains a follow-up: it is a stateful object of `XdmfTimeSeriesWriter`'s shape, not something a `(path, mesh)` writer can express.

**Note on `mdpa`:** Kratos MDPA gained a C++ reader/writer (v9.0.0, `src/cpp/src/formats/mdpa.cpp`, registered in `registry.cpp`), which is what makes it reachable from WASM/C API/Fortran/Julia/R/native CLI — but **the Python `mdpa.read` deliberately stays on the pure-Python reference**, unlike every other shim in the repo. That is not an oversight: `_mdpa.py` returns a *non-standard* `Mesh` whose `cell_data` is nested **by cell type** (`{cell_type: {var: array}}`, not the repo-wide `{var: [array_per_block]}`) and which carries `mesh.misc_data` (SubModelPart hierarchy, original Kratos entity ids, `Tables`/`Properties`/`Mesh`-block content) and `mesh.geometries_block` — attributes the C++ `Mesh` cannot hold and which ~24 tests in `tests/python/test_mdpa.py` assert. A C++-first read shim would silently change all of that, so `read` is Python-only and `write` is the C++/fallback shim, gated on the mesh carrying no MDPA extras (`misc_data`/`geometries_block`/`field_data`). The C++ reader maps Nodes/Elements/Conditions (+ property ids as `gmsh:physical`), `ModelPartData` (numeric only), `NodalData`/`ElementalData`/`ConditionalData` and SubModelParts → `Point`/`Cell` **regions** (nested parts flattened to a `parent/child` name), and **throws `ReadError` naming the construct** for everything it cannot represent, so the Python fallback stays correct rather than lossy. **v9.1.0 shortened that list and made the rest optional:** a non-empty `Properties` body is now *parsed* (unconditionally -- a pure de-throwing, so no read that used to succeed changed) into the `MdpaInfo` side channel, and `ReadOptions::mLenient` downgrades the remainder (`Table`/`Geometries`/`Mesh`/`Constraints`, non-empty `SubModelPartData`/`Tables`, a non-numeric `ModelPartData` value) to a warn-and-skip recorded in `MdpaInfo::mSkippedConstructs`. A duplicate node id, a malformed row and an unknown entity name still throw **even under `mLenient`** -- skipping those returns a quietly *wrong* mesh, not merely an incomplete one, which is the line that flag must not cross. **v9.13.0 removed non-sequential node ids from that list, in both readers.** Ids may now be arbitrary -- gapped and non-monotonic, which is what SubModelPart extraction / entity removal / deck merging leave behind, and exactly the input `model_part.hpp`'s id-keyed `ModelPart` exists for -- resolved through a file-id → row map built **lazily** on the first id that is not `row + 1` (`abaqus.cpp`'s `mPointIds` / `unv.cpp`'s `label_to_index`, minus their unconditional cost: a `1..n` deck, i.e. the million-node ones, never leaves the arithmetic path and reads byte-identically to before). Deliberately **not** gated on `mLenient` -- accepting arbitrary ids is strictly more *correct*, not more lenient, the same "pure de-throwing" argument the v9.1.0 `Properties` body used -- which is the whole point, since WASM/C API/Fortran/Julia/R/the native CLI have no Python to fall back to and so could not read a real gapped deck at all. Three facts worth not rediscovering: the C++ reader stages **raw file ids** in `MdpaBlock::mConn` and resolves them in the materialize pass rather than in the parse loop, which keeps `Begin Elements` before `Begin Nodes` working (it always did) and keeps the *file* id available for the error message; a **bare `x y z` row takes its position as its id**, which is what makes a mixed block well-defined and an id-less file byte-identical to before (that form was entirely untested until now -- the single riskiest regression in the change); and the Python twin's `_parse_generic_data_block` was resolving nodal rows as `id - 1` while **ignoring the map it was handed**, so `node_id_map` at `_mdpa.py`'s NodalData branch was dead weight -- fixing that is what makes `NodalData` keyed by real ids, and `test_gapped_nodal_data_keyed_by_file_id` was verified to fail against the old logic before it landed. **v9.14.0 closed the write half, and with it the MDPA section of the roadmap in full.** Both writers now honour `point_data`/`cell_data[kMdpaIdName]` (`"mdpa:id"`, the `med:num` `<format>:<thing>` convention) when the mesh carries it -- writing the *original* node/element/condition ids back instead of unconditionally renumbering -- and both readers attach it, but **only when it matters**: exactly when the file's ids were not already the trivial `1..n` a fresh write would produce anyway (the same lazy-map trigger v9.13.0 introduced for reading), so a sequential deck picks up no `mdpa:id` at all and a re-write of it stays on the byte-identical old code path. Every place a node/entity is referenced -- connectivity, `NodalData`/`ElementalData`/`ConditionalData` row keys, `SubModelPart` lists -- resolves through the *same written id*, never a bare `+ 1`; getting only the `Nodes` block itself right while leaving connectivity on `+ 1` was the first, wrong shape this took during development, caught immediately by a round-trip re-read throwing `"connectivity refers to node id 1, which the file's Nodes block does not define"` -- the reason every test here re-reads the written file rather than only inspecting it. A duplicate id in either array is a `WriteError` (checked separately for elements vs conditions, which have independent Kratos id namespaces), and a missing/wrong-length/wrong-block-count array falls back to the old renumbering rather than partially trusting stale metadata. **This surfaced a real, pre-existing correctness bug and fixed it**: the Python reference writer had always re-emitted `SubModelPartElements`/`Conditions` membership using the *raw original* ids stored at read time, which was already wrong whenever a plain read→write renumbered entities (the universal case before this feature) or reclassified one across the Elements/Conditions boundary (`_compute_blocks_name`'s dimension heuristic, reproducible on `tests/python/input/mdpa/test_submodelparts_hierarchical.mdpa`'s lone `LineCondition3D2N`) -- silently emitting a `SubModelPart` reference to an id absent from (or now belonging to a different block kind in) the very file being written. The fix resolves each raw id through `misc_data["reader_element_ids_info"]`/`["reader_condition_ids_info"]` (captured at read time: original id → `(meshio_type, local_idx)`) and then through the writer's own `mdpa_written_entity_ids` (that key → the id actually written this time), warning and dropping an id that fails to resolve rather than emitting it dangling; a mesh with no reader info at all falls back to the old verbatim behaviour. `Begin Mesh`'s `MeshElements`/`Conditions` (not `SubModelPart`) are a narrower, **deliberately unfixed** exception: an existing test (`test_roundtrip_all_blocks`) pins them writing the raw id verbatim so two independent reads' `misc_data` compare equal, so that path keeps its documented behaviour rather than changing as a side effect; `MeshNodes` (a plain node reference) does get the preserved-id treatment, since nothing tests the old behaviour there. C++ needed no equivalent fix -- its `SubModelPart` writer has always resolved through `written_ids`/region indices computed in the same write call, so it was self-consistent by construction. **`MdpaInfo`** (the `MedInfo`/`ExodusInfo` pattern -- `read_mdpa(path, info)` / `write_mdpa(path, mesh, info)`, both additive overloads) exists because `NDArray` has ten numeric dtypes and no string one, so `CONSTITUTIVE_LAW LinearElastic3DLaw` has no `field_data` representation at all; it carries the `Properties` bodies as `PropertyValue` key/value pairs from the new backend-neutral `properties.hpp` (shared with `ModelPart`, so there is one representation) -- typed where numeric, **verbatim text otherwise, which is what makes an unrecognized value lossless** -- plus the per-block Kratos entity names, without which a round trip renames every `SmallDisplacementElement3D4N` to `Element3D4N`. The reader's block-splitting key gained the entity name, so two adjacent same-type differently-named blocks stay separate. **v9.2.0 moved the `Properties` bodies onto the `Mesh`** (the uniform-API property sets above), because the `MdpaInfo` side channel was unreachable from `registry_read` -- `ReadFn`/`ReadExFn`/`WriteFn` have no info slot -- so every registry-based consumer, i.e. all of them, got ids and no material data, and a registry-driven `mdpa`->`mdpa` write emitted empty blocks. `read_mdpa` now stores them on the mesh unconditionally, `KratosMesh::Materialize` fills `ModelPart::Properties` with **real values** instead of id-only sets (which is what makes `to_model_part`'s applier transfer anything), and the writer emits the mesh's sets plus an empty block per referenced-but-uncovered id. `MdpaInfo` is retained and still **wins** when supplied: it preserves the blocks' *file order*, which the mesh channel deliberately does not (it canonicalizes to ascending id), and it carries `mEntityNames`/`mSkippedConstructs`. A mesh with no property sets writes byte-identical output (pinned by a test). The **writer** now declares every properties id its rows reference instead of one hard-coded `Properties 0` (the rows always wrote their `gmsh:physical` value, so a tagged mesh referenced undeclared properties, which Kratos's own `ModelPartIO` rejects); output stays byte-identical whenever every id is 0, i.e. for every mesh with no `gmsh:physical`. Entity names resolve through **`backends/kratos_names.hpp`**'s `cell_type_from_kratos_name_or_suffix` (never re-transcribe those tables) -- exact lookup then longest resolving suffix, which is what makes real decks' `SmallDisplacementElement3D4N` work. Since v9.1.0 that helper is the **single owner** of the rule and `ModelPart::CreateNewElement` goes through it too, so the two cannot disagree; before that, `CreateNewElement("SmallDisplacementElement3D4N", ...)` threw, which is what essentially every real Kratos application calls. the hexahedron20/27 Kratos↔VTK permutation is applied on read and undone on write (scatter `out[table[i]] = in[i]` reading, gather `out[j] = in[table[j]]` writing — the argsort pair the Python reference builds).

**Note on `med`:** the C++ core (built with HDF5) handles the mesh-representation part of MED exactly — points, point/cell tags, families with `GRO` group names, mesh-level metadata (`mesh_name`/`description`/`unit_time`/`unit_coords`/`point_tag_groups`/`cell_tag_groups`, via `MedInfo`), node-orientation permutations, and `POG`/`POG2` ragged polygons — and `meshioplusplus.med.read`/`write` use it by default. **v9.6.0 promoted MED into the named-regions Phase 1 list** (see the "Named regions" entry above and [`doc/formats/med.md`](doc/formats/med.md#named-regions)): `med_attach_point_regions`/`med_attach_cell_regions` (`formats/med.cpp`) derive one `Region` per group *name* from the same family tables `MedInfo` already carries, alongside — not instead of — `point_tags`/`cell_tags`, so this needed no `MedInfo` schema change and no binding change at all (`py_regions_to_mesh`/`regions_to_py` in `np_conversions.hpp` already carry `.regions` for every format, MED included). On write, `med_point_regions_to_tags`/`med_cell_regions_to_tags` synthesize families from `Point`/`Cell` regions — one family per unique combination of region names, node ids positive from `+1`/element ids negative from `-1`, a C++ port of `_ensure_med_families`'s algorithm matched step for step so the two produce byte-identical `FAS`/`FAM` output — but **only when the mesh carries no native `point_tags`/`cell_tags` of its own** (native data always wins, so a MED→MED round trip is unaffected). This closes a real pre-v9.6.0 bug: a regions-only mesh (e.g. read from Abaqus, no native MED tags) written through the C++ path used to produce a file with **no** `FAS` groups at all, because nothing about the write ever raised to trigger the Python `_ensure_med_families` bridging — `registry.cpp`'s MED writer entry, which supplies an empty `MedInfo`, now closes that gap for free. `Side` regions have no MED equivalent and are dropped with a `log::warn`. The same release added the optional **`NUM`** global node/element numbering Salome/Code_Aster/Kratos write, carried as `point_data`/`cell_data["med:num"]` (the `gmsh:physical`-style `<format>:<thing>` convention, chosen over `MedInfo` specifically because the flat bindings can see it; cell `NUM` only when *every* block has it, else dropped with a warning rather than fabricated), and an **`INFOS_GENERALES` version check** — `MAJ > 4` raises a named `ReadError`, twinned identically in the C++ reader and `_med.py` so a file rejected by one is rejected by the other rather than silently misread by whichever path runs second. `line4`/`SE4` was also added to the type table (no permutation, 1-D). The Python shim's `read()` no longer re-derives `point_sets`/`cell_sets` from the returned tags after a successful C++ read — `mesh_to_py` already attached the equivalent regions, and re-assigning through the property setters would only discard the `dim`/`tag` `mesh_to_py` set. Since v8.7.0 it also handles **`CHA` fields (point_data/cell_data) for the single-timestep common case**: `write_cha_nodal_field`/`write_cha_cell_field` (`formats/med.cpp`) write one `NOE` (nodal) or one-or-more `MAI.<type>` (per contributing cell block) support subgroup per field, fixed `ndt=1/nor=-1/pdt=0.0`, blank `UNI`/`UNT`/`NOM`, and **no MED-4.1 optimization bitmask** (`LEN`/`LGC`/`LNA`/`LAA` — our own reader never reads it, so its absence costs nothing for a meshio++ round-trip, only for interop with tools like Salome/MEDCoupling that use it) — a deliberate, documented scope cut, not an oversight. `read_cha_fields` is the mirror: it accepts only that exact shape and **declines (throws, deferring the whole file to Python) rather than silently drop information** whenever a field's `UNI`/`UNT` is non-empty or a timestep's `NDT`/`NOR`/`PDT` isn't the write-side default — reading a hand-edited or Python-written file with real units/step metadata and silently reporting `ndt=1` would be a wrong answer, not merely an incomplete one. The **enhanced-feature guard lives in the Python shim (`med/__init__.py`), not the C++ core**: `med:field_units`/`med:step_meta` are Python-only dict-valued `field_data` conventions, and `med_write`'s pybind wrapper always uses `lenient_field_data=true` (silently dropping any non-numeric `field_data` entry before it ever reaches the C++ `Mesh`) precisely *because* of conventions like this — a C++-side `HasFieldData("med:field_units")` check is structurally dead code under every binding, since none of them can construct a dict-valued field_data entry in the first place. The shim also defers when any array name uses the `"Name[idx] - pdt"` multi-timestep encoding `_parse_med_field_name` recognizes (`_names_encode_a_timestep`), which the C++ writer has no notion of and would otherwise write as unrelated separate fields instead of grouping them. It still **raises** (→ Python fallback) for what remains out of scope: those three enhanced-field cases, non-default profiles/ELGA (ELNO/ELGA is structurally unreachable regardless — the uniform mesh API's `cell_data` is always `(n,)`/`(n,k)`, never a 3-D per-node-within-cell shape), and multi-mesh files (`read_med_multi`/`write_med_multi` are always Python). The C++ MED reader iterates `MAI` cell blocks in HDF5 **creation order** (`group_links_crt`, matching h5py `track_order`) since block order aligns cell_data/cell_sets — note this means a multi-block mesh's block order does **not** necessarily survive a MED round-trip (blocks come back in whatever order the file's own `MAI` group lists them), so field values must always be looked up by **cell type**, never a fixed block index, both in the writer (`meshio_to_med()` per block) and any test of it; the pure-Python fallback reader still reconstructs `point_sets`/`cell_sets` from families via `_families_to_point_sets`/`_families_to_cell_sets` (the C++-path shim does not, per the regions paragraph above). **v9.8.0 closed the two remaining gaps that made a real Gmsh-4.1-sourced mesh unwritable to MED from every flat binding (WASM/C API/Fortran, none of which has Python to fall back to):** the `gmsh:physical`→family bridging is now **native in C++**, not deferred — `med_cell_regions_to_tags` folds `cell_data["gmsh:physical"]` (via `field_data`-derived names, else `"group_<id>"`, skipping an id already covered by a named `Cell` region) into the same per-cell name-set combo pass as `Point`/`Cell` regions, a direct C++ port of `_ensure_med_families`'s cell-side bridging; and **same-type cell blocks are consolidated on write** instead of throwing `"MED files cannot have two sections of the same cell type."` — MSH 4.1's canonical structure is one cell block per *entity*, so a real 4.1 file routinely has several same-type blocks. The cell-writing loop now groups blocks by type (first-seen order) and writes one `MAI/<type>` section per type, concatenating connectivity (one `flatten_f` pass over the merged `(n,k)` array, mirroring `_med.py`'s `np.concatenate` + single `.flatten(order="F")` — a per-block flatten-then-concat is **not** the same array) and `FAM`/`NUM` (row-concatenated, written only when every contributing block is covered by the source data, else `log::warn`-dropped for that section rather than partially written); `write_cha_cell_field` groups by type the same way so two same-type blocks share one `MAI.<type>` CHA support subgroup instead of colliding on group creation. Neither change altered `_med.py`'s own output (the Python reference already merged same-type blocks and bridged `gmsh:physical`, since always going through the Python fallback) — only the C++ path's behavior, which now matches it. **v9.9.0 closed the remaining "handled by Python fallback" surface with `ReadOptions::mLenient`** (the `mdpa` mechanism, v9.1.0) and registered MED in **`registry_readers_ex()`** -- which is the entire cross-binding cost, since that table is what carries options to WASM/C API/Fortran/Julia/R/both CLIs with no per-binding code. **Strict reads are byte-identical to v9.8.0's**, deliberately: the Python shim still falls back, so the Python surface does not move; lenient is the only way a Python-less binding gets through a real Salome/Code_Aster file at all. Constructs that can be *described* are read **into `MedInfo`** rather than merely skipped -- `mFieldUnits` (`UNI`/`UNT`), `mStepMeta` (non-default `NDT`/`NOR`/`PDT`), `mFieldTimeValues` (every step's `PDT`), `mSkippedConstructs` -- and only the structurally unrepresentable ones (named `PFL` profile, `ELNO`/`ELGA` support, a field mixing nodal and cell supports) drop that one field with a warn. **ELNO/ELGA is structurally impossible, not unimplemented**: the uniform API's `cell_data` is always `(n,)`/`(n,k)`, never per-node-within-cell 3-D, so no work in this format closes it. `MedInfo` gaining four fields is a **Tier A ABI change** (layout), which is why v9.9.0 bumps `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_ABI_VERSION` 4 -> 5 -- the one release-note item a C++ consumer must act on. `ReadOptions::mTimeStep` selects one step of a multi-step `CHA` field (MED is the third reader to honour it, after exodus and XDMF; step order comes from the zero-padded `(NDT,NOR)` subgroup names, so name order *is* step order) and is honoured **without** `mLenient` -- a request the shim never makes, so no Python behaviour depends on it. A block with **no `FAM` array now reads as family 0** (MED's own spelling of "no family") instead of failing the file; the Python twin was wrong here differently -- it appended nothing, leaving `cell_tags` *shorter* than `mesh.cells`, a shape the uniform API cannot hold -- and now zero-fills identically. See [`doc/formats/med.md`](doc/formats/med.md#lenient-reads).

**Note on `dolfin`:** DOLFIN XML stores each data array in a **sibling file** `<stem>_<name>.xml` holding one `<mesh_function>`, and that element's **`dim` attribute is the topological dimension of the entities it is defined on** -- so `dim="0"` means *vertices*. v9.9.0 uses exactly that as the point/cell discriminator, which is why adding `point_data` (previously dropped on write, in both the C++ core and the Python twin) needed **no new file convention** -- the format already had one. `cell_data` had always round-tripped through the same mechanism and is untouched; its `dim` is a pre-existing **z-flatness heuristic** (2 if the mesh is 2-D or every z is exactly 0, else 3), kept for compatibility and never `0`, which is what makes the split unambiguous. Two collisions are warn-and-skipped rather than silently resolved: a name present in **both** locations wants the same sibling file (cell data owns it, having always had it), and a **non-scalar** `point_data` array has nowhere to put its extra components. The **triangle/tetrahedron-only restriction is correct by format** -- DOLFIN XML is simplicial -- and both writers raise a named `WriteError`, so it is not a defect to close. The gtest asserts `dim="0"` in the **raw written bytes**, not by reading our own output back: the latter passes even with a wrong `dim`, since the reader would just route the array to `cell_data` and the values would still be there -- the same "a format's own reader is not an oracle for its writer" shape as the CGNS ordering suite.

**Note on `openfoam`:** read-only until v9.20.0, when it gained a **writer** — the only one in meshio++ that **creates a directory** (`<case>/constant/polyMesh/`, five files, plus the empty `.foam` marker when the target has that extension). It is built on **`detail/face_mesh.hpp`**'s `build_global_faces`, which re-expresses a mesh's volume cells as a globally deduplicated face list with owner/neighbour pairing — the shape a polyMesh *is* on disk, and the shape CGNS's `NGON_n`+`NFACE_n` pair is too, which is why cell rows are stored in CGNS's own **signed 1-based** encoding (`+(f+1)` = as stored, `-(f+1)` = reversed) rather than in anything OpenFOAM-specific. Three facts there are load-bearing: cells are numbered in a **compact volume-cell space, not `block_bases`' block-major one** (every mesh this reader produces carries its boundary faces as 2D blocks, so global cell 8 is routinely a quad — `mCellToGlobal` bridges back for `cell_data`); winding is **repaired unconditionally** via `orient_rings`, because `cell_faces.hpp`'s rows are outward on the *reference* element and an inverted hexahedron would otherwise emit six inward normals that `checkMesh` rejects one by one; and one `detail::FacetKey` serves both tabulated and polyhedral cells, which is what lets a hexahedron and a polyhedron meeting on a face meet on **one** face. Determinism is `surface.cpp`'s phase split (parallel fill into disjoint slots, then a **serial** first-seen dedup in ascending (compact cell, local face) order), and `owner < neighbour` *falls out* of that order — but it is a derivation, so the validator checks it anyway. **The ordering contract is the part that silently produces a mesh `checkMesh` rejects**: `neighbour` holds only internal faces (our own reader also accepts a `-1`-padded one, which is exactly why round-tripping through it is a **weak** oracle for this writer), internal faces first, sorted strictly by `(owner, neighbour)` — with the **face id as a third sort key, not decoration**, since two cells can share two distinct faces and `(owner, neighbour)` alone is then not a strict weak ordering and `std::sort` would be UB — normals pointing owner→neighbour, and patches partitioning the boundary range **with every face in a patch's range actually belonging to it** (checking only that the start/count table tiles the range is *not* enough: the table is built by counting, so it can describe a contiguity the face order does not have — a hole found by sabotage, since the C6 test passed without the sort until the validator was tightened). All of it is re-validated before any file is opened, in **release builds too**. ASCII output has one trap: the **count must be on a line of its own** — `parse_*_ascii` takes "the first line that is entirely digits" as the count, so `8(` reads back as an **empty list**, not an error. Two **reader** fixes came with it: `parse_boundary` now reads each patch's `type` (the *Python* reader always did — the C++ one was behind) and matches braces by **depth** rather than taking the first `}`, without which any patch carrying a nested sub-dictionary (`cyclicAMI`'s `transform`, `mappedWall`'s `sample`) was truncated into garbage patches; the second is not optional given the first, or `type` would be read from a truncated block. `OpenFoamInfo` gained **`mPatchTypes`** (family id → type, the *same* negative key as `mCellTags` so the two cannot be mis-joined) — a **Tier A** change, hence `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_ABI_VERSION` 5→6, and the occasion to finally pin `OpenFoamInfo`/`GmshInfo`/`MdpaInfo` in `test_abi_layout.cpp`, which pinned **no** side-channel struct before (`MedInfo`/`ExodusInfo` stay unpinned: they exist only in an HDF5/netCDF build, and a layout snapshot that says different things per configuration is worse than none). Types needing companion dictionary entries the struct cannot carry (`cyclic`/`processor`/`mapped*`) are **downgraded to `patch` with a warn** — emitting them bare yields a case OpenFOAM refuses to *load* — and an unknown type writes `patch`, never `wall`, since `wall` selects wall functions and would silently change a solve's physics. A mesh with no tags gets one **`defaultFaces`** patch (blockMesh's own name); patches are never synthesized from geometry. **There is deliberately no Python fallback writer**: a twin would re-implement the winding repair, a discrete branch on a sign, and two such implementations can land on opposite sides for a near-degenerate cell (`smooth`'s inversion-guard reasoning); it would also be dead code, since `openfoam_write` needs no optional dep and ships in every wheel — so the writer is registered only when `_core` provides it, and `.foam` joined `registry_extension_defaults()` (before this, `resolve_format` **threw** on `.foam`, so the flat bindings could not read a case by extension at all). Tests read the written bytes with a parser deliberately independent of `read_openfoam`; the strongest available oracle is per-cell face closure + the divergence-theorem volume computed from those bytes, and `checkMesh` (env-gated, `MESHIO_OPENFOAM_CHECKMESH`) is the only thing that could catch a *globally* inverted convention.

**Note on `cgns`:** rewritten in v9.8.0 from a private tetrahedra-only encoding to a genuine CGNS/SIDS-compliant unstructured-mesh subset (`src/cpp/src/formats/cgns.cpp` + Python twin `cgns/_cgns.py`), readable by cgnslib/ParaView/VTK. The old writer emitted **only the first `tetra` block it found** (`break` on the first match) and created empty `ElementRange`/`ElementConnectivity` groups otherwise, so any non-tetra mesh — every surface/2-D mesh, which is most of what a real FE mesher produces — wrote a file this library's own reader rejected with `HDF5: missing dataset ' data'` (the leading-space name is a red herring: `#define D_DATA " data"` in cgnslib's `ADFH.c` is the real ADF-over-HDF5 mapping, not — as the pre-v9.8.0 docs here and in `doc/formats/cgns.md` wrongly claimed — an ad hoc convention). Every CGNS node is now a real ADF node: fixed-length 33/33/3-byte NULLTERM `name`/`label`/`type` attributes plus a 1-element int32 `flags` attribute (`cgns_write_attr_str`/`cgns_write_attr_flags`, modelled on `med.cpp`'s `write_attr_bytes` but NULLTERM/fixed-size rather than NULLPAD), and the file plus every group is created with **HDF5 link+attribute creation-order tracking** (`cgns_create_file`/`cgns_create_group`, `H5P_CRT_ORDER_TRACKED | H5P_CRT_ORDER_INDEXED` on the FCPL/GCPL; Python via h5py's `track_order=True`) — load-bearing, not cosmetic, since cgnslib's `has_child`/`has_data` (`ADFH.c`) iterate creation order with **no name-order fallback**. The tree is `CGNSLibraryVersion` → `Base` (`CGNSBase_t`, `" data"=[CellDim, PhysDim]`) → `Zone1` (`Zone_t`, `" data"` shape `(3,1)=[NVertex, NCell, 0]`) → `ZoneType`("Unstructured") + `GridCoordinates` + one `Elements_t` section **per meshio++ cell block** (not consolidated by type, unlike MED — CGNS has no same-type restriction), named `"<CGNS type>_<n>"` with contiguous 1-based `ElementRange`s. `CellDim`/`NCell` count only cells at the mesh's max topological dimension (boundary sections are not zone cells, per SIDS); `PhysDim` follows `max(PointDim(), CellDim)` clamped to `[1,3]`, and the reader now returns points shaped `(n, d)` for however many `CoordinateX/Y/Z` a file actually has (≥2) instead of always `(n,3)`, so a 2-D-authored file round-trips its point shape. The meshio++↔CGNS `ElementType_t` table (`cgns_type_table()` in C++, `_CGNS_TYPES` in Python — kept in exact sync) covers every fixed-node-count type through `hexahedron27`/`pyramid13`; permutations (self-inverse, `dst[c]=src[p[c]]` on both read shift=-1 and write shift=+1, `med_node_perm()`'s convention) are derived from the SIDS edge/face tables cross-checked against VTK's real translator source (`vtkWedge`/`vtkTriQuadraticHexahedron`), **not** against this repo's own `detail/cell_faces.cpp` (used for `extract_surface`/skin, a different concern) — that file's `hexahedron27` face-centre table carried its own 3-cycle defect, found while deriving the CGNS permutation and left unfixed in v9.8.0, then **fixed in lockstep in v9.9.0** (see the hexahedron27 entry below). Two orientation findings worth not rediscovering: **`wedge` is the identity permutation** (meshio++'s wedge already matches SIDS `PENTA_6` face-for-face; do **not** apply `vtk_common.hpp`'s `meshio_to_vtk_order("wedge")` `{0,2,1,3,5,4}` flip, which targets `vtkWedge`, a different convention), and **`hexahedron27`**'s mid-face order follows the real `vtkTriQuadraticHexahedron::Faces` table (`20=(0,4,7,3)`, `21=(1,2,6,5)`, `22=(0,1,5,4)`, `23=(3,7,6,2)`, `24`=bottom, `25`=top). The cubic/quartic Lagrange families (`line4`, `triangle10`, `hexahedron64`, …) are **deliberately unsupported** — SIDS shows their interior-node order only in image-only figures with no text source, and VTK's own tables for them target `VTK_LAGRANGE_*`, not meshio++'s convention — writing/reading one is a named `WriteError`/`ReadError` ("refusing to write a guessed ordering" / "not yet verified"), never a guess. `polygon`/`polyhedron*` (ragged) are also unsupported (CGNS's `MIXED`/`NGON_n`/`NFACE_n`, never written), checked by **type name**, not `IsRagged()`/`isinstance(data, list)` — a *uniform* polygon block stores rectangularly and so is not, structurally, ragged, but still has no CGNS representation, and the message must say so rather than falling through to the generic "ordering not verified" one. Backward compatible: the reader's spec/legacy discriminator is structural (a root child with `label="CGNSBase_t"`), so a pre-v9.8.0 file (or one from upstream `meshio`, same historical layout) still reads via a near-verbatim `cgns_read_legacy`/`_read_legacy` fallback; nothing writes that layout anymore. Verification without a `cgnslib` dependency (none was available in the environment this was developed in): C++ `tests/cpp/test_cgns.cpp`'s `CgnsOrdering` suite builds a reference element with nodes at their true SIDS-defined positions (computed independently of the permutation tables — corner/edge-midpoint/face-centre coordinates) and asserts the raw written file bytes place each node where SIDS says it belongs (this is what actually caught mistakes during development — a round trip through only this library's own reader+writer cannot, since the self-inverse permutation makes `read(write(m))==m` even if the table is wrong), plus a pure-computation involution check (`p[p[c]]==c`) extracted from a freshly-written file's raw bytes, independent of `read_cgns`. `tests/python/test_cgns.py::test_structural_parity_with_cpp` asserts the C++ and Python writers agree byte-for-byte except `" hdf5version"` (records the *linked* HDF5 library's own version, which legitimately differs between the C++ core and h5py). **v9.9.0 closed both open items.** (1) **`FlowSolution_t`** (point/cell data), which the format previously dropped silently: one `FlowSolution_t` per location (`GridLocation` = `Vertex`/`CellCenter`, absent ⇒ `Vertex` per SIDS), one `DataArray_t` per scalar. **CGNS has NO component concept** — no `NumberOfComponents` anywhere in the SIDS — so a k-component array is split into k siblings named `<name>_0..<name>_{k-1}` and re-joined on read from a *contiguous* `0..k-1` run (anything else — a lone `foo_7`, a gap — stays a scalar under its literal name; guessing would invent components). A documented meshio++ convention, the `zstd`-as-extension precedent. Two structural constraints that are not arbitrary: a `CellCenter` array is per-*zone* while meshio++'s `cell_data` is per-*block* and only blocks at `CellDim` are zone cells, so `cell_data` is written **only when every block is at `CellDim`** (a mixed-dimension mesh is warn-and-skipped — there is no way to distribute a zone-wide array back across blocks of different dimension without inventing values) and split back by each section's cell count in `ElementRange` order on read; and `FlowSolution_t` is **read only for a single-zone file**, since across zones the arrays would have to be concatenated in listing order and a solution on only some zones has no defensible filler. `field_data` has no CGNS home (neither per-vertex nor per-cell) and is not written. (2) **The external-validation layer**: `cgnslib` is not in apt on a sudo-less machine but **is on conda-forge** (`cgns 4.5.2`), installed into the same gitignored `.micromamba` root the xcpp/r-notebooks kernels use. `cgnscheck -w3` reports **zero errors** on everything meshio++ writes (`test_cgnscheck_accepts_our_output`, gated on `shutil.which("cgnscheck")` so it skips with an actionable reason rather than silently passing), and a reference `.cgns` **written end to end by cgnslib itself** (via `cgnsconvert -h -f`, so the committed bytes are cgnslib's) lives under `tests/python/meshes/cgns/` with a provenance README; reading it runs unconditionally. `*.cgns` was added to `.gitattributes` for LFS. The warnings `cgnscheck` does emit are style recommendations — no `Family_t`, no `DataClass_t`, "not a CGNS data-name identifier" for a field name that is not one of SIDS's standard names — not conformance failures; meshio++ keeps the caller's own field names. **v9.21.0 added `NGON_n`/`NFACE_n` — polyhedral cells — hand-rolled in BOTH directions**, so polyhedral CGNS works in the default build, the wheels and WASM rather than only under the cgnslib flag; cgnslib stays the answer for ADF containers and for the **CGNS 3.x** section layout, which the hand-rolled reader refuses **by name** (its inline per-row length prefix is exactly what `cg_poly_elements_read` exists to normalize, and guessing would misread). Hand-rolling the write side is what forced the read side: writing has no 3.x-vs-4.0 split to absorb (the writer picks its layout), but a build that could write a file it cannot read back would be worse than either extreme. `NGON_n` is emitted **before** the `NFACE_n` sections pointing into it, and its faces are deduplicated across the **polyhedral blocks only** via a block-filtered overload of `detail::build_global_faces` — a mesh mixing hexahedra with polyhedra keeps ordinary `HEXA_8` sections, and putting their faces in the pool would leave `NGON_n` elements no cell references. On read an `NGON_n` becomes `polygon<N>` blocks **only when no `NFACE_n` references it**; otherwise it is the shared face pool and emitting it as cells would double every polyhedron's geometry while leaving the cell count plausible. **Two bugs here were found only by `cgnscheck`, and both made cgnslib corrupt its own heap and abort rather than report anything** — so the tests assert the *return code*, not just the absence of `ERROR` lines: (1) `NCell` omitted the polyhedral blocks, since `cell_type_from_name` does not know `polygon<N>`/`polyhedron<N>` and they reported dimension 0, and cgnscheck sizes its cell arrays from `NCell` before reading `NFACE_n`; (2) the file declared `CGNSLibraryVersion` **3.1**, below which cgnslib reads `NGON_n` with the 3.x layout and spliced our `ElementStartOffset` array into the connectivity — a face-based file now declares **4.0**, while a file without such a section reads identically under either number and keeps 3.1 so its bytes are unchanged (pinned by `CgnsPoly.AFaceBasedFileDeclaresCgns40`, sabotage-verified). The **h5py twin deliberately does not implement any of this** and says so by name: the writer dedups faces and repairs each cell's winding, a discrete branch on the sign of an enclosed volume, and two implementations of such a branch can land on opposite sides for a near-degenerate cell (`smooth`'s inversion-guard reasoning) — which also keeps `test_structural_parity_with_cpp` exact. `cgns_write`'s pybind binding needed `allow_ragged=true`, without which a polyhedral mesh never reached the C++ writer at all.

**Note on `gid`:** read/write since v10.19.0. **Writing** rides a vendored hardcopy of CIMNE's gidpost 2.14 (`src/cpp/third_party/gidpost/`, BSD-2-Clause-Views — a hardcopy like `pugixml`, not a submodule like `eigen`/`json`, since the point is that it travels with the repo; see that directory's `README.meshioplusplus.md` for exactly what was excluded and why, notably gidpost's own Fortran binding and its `cfortran.h`-dependent file, both under a separate third-party distribution policy meshio++ has its own C-API-based Fortran module regardless). **Hard-gated on zlib, not by preference**: `gidpostInt.h` includes `<zlib.h>` unconditionally and the binary flavour is always deflated, so `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_WITH_GIDPOST` (default ON) auto-disables — a `STATUS` message, never a configure-time error, since a from-source build can always pass `--without-zlib` on purpose — whenever zlib is unavailable; compiled out, `write_gid` still exists and throws naming both `-DMESHIOPLUSPLUS_WITH_GIDPOST=ON`/`-DMESHIOPLUSPLUS_WITH_ZLIB=ON` (the `partition_kahip_parts` contract), so `.post.msh` can never silently resolve to another format. The vendored `.c` files join `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_CORE_SOURCES` directly (not a separate target) specifically so they inherit Emscripten's `-sUSE_ZLIB=1` on `meshioplusplus_core_obj` — a separate target would not see it and would fail to find `<zlib.h>` under WASM at all. **A second, independent HDF5 probe exists for gidpost's own HDF5 flavour**: `gidpost`'s `hdf5c.c` calls `H5LT*` (the HDF5 *high-level* library) directly, which none of the other HDF5-backed formats here need, so `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_HDF5_HL_LIBRARY` (a plain `find_library(hdf5_hl)`, hinted from `HDF5_C_LIBRARIES`' own directory) is a deliberately **separate, best-effort** probe rather than widening the main `find_package(HDF5 COMPONENTS C)` call to add `HL` — requesting an unavailable component there can flip the whole-package `HDF5_FOUND` false, which would silently disable CGNS/HMF/H5M/MED/XDMF-HDF for every user on a system missing `hdf5_hl`, a blast radius far beyond gidpost's own `hdf5` flavour (which `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_HAS_GIDPOST_HDF5` gates instead — absent HL, the writer still builds, just without `GidMode::Hdf5`). One CMake gotcha bit this during development and is worth not rediscovering: `find_library(VAR ...)` populates a *cache* entry, and a preceding plain `set(VAR "")` of the *same name* shadows that cache entry for every later read in the directory scope even though `find_library` still (successfully) writes it — the fix is simply never pre-setting the variable, letting `find_library`'s own `NOTFOUND` default do the job. **Node ordering is identity for every currently-supported type** (`vertex`/`line`/`line3`/`triangle`/`triangle6`/`quad`/`quad8`/`quad9`/`tetra`/`tetra10`/`hexahedron`/`hexahedron20`/`wedge`/`pyramid`) — independently cross-checked against Kratos Multiphysics's own production GiD writer (`kratos/includes/gid_mesh_container.h`), whose only reordering is for `hexahedron20`, and deriving *why* that reorder exists is what settled every other type: Kratos's own internal hexahedra20 node order (corners, bottom ring, verticals, top ring) differs from GiD's (corners, bottom ring, top ring, verticals) for reasons specific to Kratos's own geometry convention, and GiD's own order turns out to be, edge for edge, identical to meshio++'s own `hexahedron20` table (`detail/cell_subdivision.cpp`'s `cell_refine_edges`) — so the meshio++→GiD mapping needs **no permutation at all**, not even a self-inverse one. Kratos's own `triangle_2d_6.h`/`tetrahedra_3d_10.h`/`quadrilateral_2d_8.h` apply no reorder when writing to GiD either, and their edge tables match meshio++'s edge-for-edge too. `hexahedron27`/`wedge15` extend that cross-check to Kratos's `hexahedra_3d_27.h`/`prism_3d_15.h` geometry classes -- their internal node order splits mid-edge nodes as bottom-ring/VERTICALS/top-ring (the prism equivalent: bottom-tri/VERTICALS/top-tri), the reverse pairing of meshio++'s own bottom-ring/top-ring/verticals, so unlike hexahedron20 these two genuinely need a self-inverse permutation (`dst[c]=src[p[c]]`, the `med_node_perm()` convention, shared between writer and reader in `gid_common.hpp`'s `gid_cell_perm_table()`) -- hexahedron27 additionally permutes its six face-centre nodes (body centre unaffected). Neither has a Conditions-only-swap precedent the way hexahedron20 does, so both cross-check instead against Kratos's Element-agnostic `vtk_output.cpp`/`ensight_output.cpp` Kratos-to-VTK conversion utilities, which independently reproduce the identical swaps -- the SAME broader source that, in retrospect, is the more precise citation for hexahedron20's own resolution too (the `gid_mesh_container.h` reorder that resolution originally cited turns out to live only in that file's Conditions-writing path, not Elements, though the conclusion is unaffected since vtk_output.cpp confirms it independently). `pyramid13` needs NO permutation -- Kratos's own `Pyramid3D13` order already matches meshio++'s, confirmed by `vtk_output.cpp` explicitly skipping any conversion for it -- and it is the one type of the three with NO Kratos-GiD precedent at all, since Kratos never registers a GiD mesh container for any pyramid; its ordering rests on Kratos's internal geometry convention alone. `polygon`/`polyhedron` (GiD has no such type) throw too. Pinned by `tests/cpp/test_gid.cpp`'s `GidOrdering` suite, which builds a cell at geometrically-known corner/edge-midpoint positions with identity connectivity, writes it, then **re-parses the raw written file** (a from-scratch, from-first-principles text parser, never any meshio++ reading code) and checks where each referenced node id's coordinates actually are — the bytes-oracle pattern `cgns.cpp`'s `CgnsOrdering` suite established, necessarily so here since gidpost has no reader to round-trip through at all. One further wiring bug worth recording: `GiD_fWriteElementsIdBlock`/`_IdMatBlock` already wrap `BeginElements()`/`EndElements()` internally (gidpost's own header says so), so an *additional* explicit `GiD_fBeginElements`/`_fEndElements` pair around them silently double-nests the `Elements`/`End Elements` markers in the output — caught by writing a real file and inspecting it by eye during development, not by any test (the parser tolerates the duplication), which is itself a reminder that a bytes oracle checks the specific claim it was built for and nothing else. Three on-disk flavours, chosen by `GidMode` or inferred from the path (`GidMode::Auto`): **ascii** = two sibling files, `<stem>.post.msh` (geometry) + `<stem>.post.res` (results), two gidpost handles; **binary**/**hdf5** = one file, one handle (`GiD_fOpenPostResultFile` alone, whose handle is *also* used for the mesh-writing calls — gidpost never calls `GiD_fOpenPostMeshFile` in these two modes). Only the **first** mesh writes the shared coordinate table; every subsequent mesh needs an explicit empty `GiD_fBeginCoordinates`/`GiD_fEndCoordinates` pair or gidpost's own state machine rejects the file (confirmed against Kratos's identical workaround). Element ids are a single running 1-based counter across **every** block, never per-block — `GiD_fWriteElementsIdBlock` numbers `1..n` *per mesh* on its own, which collides the instant a mesh has more than one block. `GiD_ResultLocation` has no "on cells" concept, so `cell_data` goes out `GiD_OnGaussPoints` against a synthetic one-point Gauss set declared once per block (`"gp_<mesh_name>"`), the standard GiD idiom (and how Kratos's own writer represents a per-element field). An integral `cell_data["gmsh:physical"]` covering every block becomes the material-id column (`GiD_fWriteElementsIdMatBlock`) and is excluded from the result file — the one documented material-id key, no others consulted. `GiD_ResultType`'s valid component counts are irregular and gidpost does not validate them itself (a wrong count silently produces a malformed file). With no declaration, meshio++ maps 1→Scalar, 2/3→Vector, and splits anything else into that many named scalars with a `provenance_note` — an undeclared 6-component array is **deliberately not** inferred as `GiD_Matrix`, since `(n,6)` alone is genuinely ambiguous (`Matrix:6`? `ComplexMatrix:3`? `ComplexVector:6`?). A caller **declares** the type instead, via a `field_data["gid:result_type:<name>"]` entry holding a `GidResultType` value (`gid.hpp`'s `kGidResultTypePrefix`) — chosen over a side-channel struct (the `MedInfo` shape) precisely because the registry's `(path, mesh)` writers cannot carry one, which would have made the declaration invisible from the CLI/WASM/C API/Fortran; a `field_data` key reaches all of them for free, as `gmsh:physical`/`med:num` already do. All nine `GiD_ResultType`s are supported: `Scalar`/`Vector`/`Matrix`/`PlainDeformationMatrix`/`MainMatrix`/`LocalAxes`/`ComplexScalar`/`ComplexVector`/`ComplexMatrix`, with legal counts and orders taken from gidpost's own `_ResultTypeInfo` and CIMNE's Customization Manual respectively, never guessed. Two facts worth not rediscovering: GiD's `Matrix:6` is `Sxx Syy Szz Sxy Syz Sxz`, which is **already** meshio/VTK's symmetric-tensor order (no permutation needed in either direction — a claim this repo previously stated backwards); and `ComplexVector` **interleaves** real/imaginary parts per component while `ComplexMatrix` **blocks** them (every real, then every imaginary) — the same family, opposite conventions, neither inferable from the other. A declaration is recorded on read **only when it carries information** (i.e. differs from what the inference above would have produced for that count), so a plain scalar/vector round trip stays free of `gid:result_type:*` noise, and a mesh with no declarations writes byte-identical output to a build without this feature. An illegal count for a declared type is a `WriteError` naming the array and the legal counts — never a silent fallback to splitting. Provenance is `SlotTier::Block`, one `GiD_fWriteMeshUserAttribute`/`GiD_fWriteResultUserAttribute` call per line (`"meshio++"` then `"meshio++_<n>"`), which gidpost itself renders as `# Name: value` in ascii/binary and an HDF5 group attribute otherwise — confirmed via raw-text inspection (ascii) and `h5py` inspection of the `Meshes/1` group's attributes (hdf5); the *result*-file attribute's exact HDF5 association is not independently verified and is a documented, low-stakes gap rather than a claimed guarantee. **There is no pure-Python reference for this format at all** (not merely one that goes unused) — gidpost cannot be cheaply reimplemented, and a second implementation of its node-ordering permutations risks disagreeing with the first near exactly the cases the bytes tests exist to pin — so `meshioplusplus.gid` is registered **unconditionally** (never gated on a "does the writer exist" probe the way `openfoam`'s shim is), because deregistering it would let `.post.msh` fall through to `.msh`'s own `ansys`/`gmsh`/`freefem` candidates instead of raising this format's own specific, actionable error. Extension dispatch needed a genuine fix on both sides for this to work at all: the C++ `resolve_format` used to take only the *last* `.`-delimited suffix (so `.post.msh` resolved to `.msh` → `gmsh`), now walks the basename from its *first* dot forward so the longest compound suffix wins; the Python `_filetypes_from_path` used to *append* each suffix's candidates (shortest-match-first), now *prepends* (longest-match-first) — proven behaviour-preserving for every extension registered before this change, since the only pre-existing compound extensions (`.vol.gz`/`.post.gz`/`.dato.gz`) have no registered bare `.gz` to collide with. `_pick_best_format`'s gmsh-tag heuristic was anchored to only fire when `gmsh`/`ansys`/`freefem` is *already* the first (specific-extension) candidate, so a `gmsh:physical`-tagged mesh written to `.post.msh` still resolves to `gid`, not `gmsh`. Reachable from every registry-driven surface (WASM/C API/Fortran/native CLI) with **zero** binding-specific code beyond the shared `registry.cpp` writer/extension-table entries — the WASM leg in particular needed nothing beyond that, since `-sUSE_ZLIB=1` was already wired for the core object library. **Since v10.20.0, the statically-linked release CLI binaries and every published wheel ship WITH `gid`**: `cli.yml` and `wheels.yml` used to build `-DMESHIOPLUSPLUS_WITH_ZLIB=OFF` on all their legs (not only Windows, as this note previously said — every `wheels.yml` OS leg shares the same `CIBW_ENVIRONMENT`), which turned the writer off along with everything else optional. An ordinary `find_package(ZLIB)` was never the fix, since it links the system libz *dynamically*, defeating the whole "one dependency-free artifact" premise those two workflows exist for. `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_ZLIB_STATIC` (a new option, OFF by default, orthogonal to `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_STATIC_RUNTIME`) is the fix: when ON, it `FetchContent_Declare`s a pinned, SHA256-checked zlib 1.3.1 release (mirroring the in-tree `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_BUILD_TESTS` GoogleTest fetch just above it, and `build-wasm-deps.sh`'s pin discipline for HDF5/netCDF/cgnslib) and links only its `zlibstatic` target (zlib's own CMakeLists.txt always builds both `zlib`/shared and `zlibstatic`/static, with no option to skip the shared one), aliased to `ZLIB::ZLIB` so every existing consumer below — including this gidpost gate and the ordinary VTU zlib branch — needed no further change; `SKIP_INSTALL_ALL`/`ZLIB_BUILD_EXAMPLES` are forced off first (the former matters specifically for the wheel build, whose `cmake --install` would otherwise also install zlib's own headers/lib/pkgconfig into the wheel prefix). Both `cli.yml` and `wheels.yml` now pass `-DMESHIOPLUSPLUS_WITH_ZLIB=ON -DMESHIOPLUSPLUS_ZLIB_STATIC=ON`, and their dependency-verification steps (Linux `ldd`, the newly-added macOS `otool -L`) assert no dynamic `libz` line appears, proving the static link actually took rather than silently falling back. **One accepted side effect, not a bug**: `registry.cpp`'s VTU/VTP writer entries already follow a "default follows the build" convention identical to XDMF's HDF-vs-XML choice (`#ifdef MESHIOPLUSPLUS_HAS_ZLIB` picks `zlib=true` over `zlib=false` for the default write), so the default `.vtu`/`.vtp` output from these two artifact classes is now zlib-compressed instead of uncompressed — still standard, universally-readable VTK XML, just different bytes than before v10.20.0 (see `CHANGELOG.md`'s **Breaking:** entry). **The reader (v10.19.0) is meshio++'s own code, not gidpost's** — that library has zero read functions — so it lives in a SEPARATE translation unit `src/cpp/src/formats/gid_read.cpp`, deliberately **outside** `gid.cpp`'s `MESHIOPLUSPLUS_HAS_GIDPOST` guard, with the three shared path helpers hoisted into a format-private `src/cpp/src/formats/gid_common.hpp` (the `xdmf_doc.hpp` precedent; a **named** namespace, not anonymous, because the amalgamation concatenates every `src/cpp/src/**.cpp` into one TU). Its real dependencies are **per flavour** — ascii needs nothing, binary and gzipped-ascii need zlib, hdf5 needs HDF5 — so `gid` is **readable in strictly more build configurations than it is writable**, and the sentence above about the release CLI binaries is exactly why that matters: they cannot write GiD but do read the ascii flavour. `registry_readers()`'s entry is therefore **unguarded**, unlike the writer's, and `gid` must NOT be listed in `registry_compiled_out()` (there is no missing dependency to name). `gid_available` keeps its released v10.18.0 meaning (**write** capability) and `gid_readable` is the read-side twin — silently redefining the former would have been a behaviour change for an existing consumer. Three grammar facts the reader is built on, none of them guessable: **`Nnode` is the ONLY disambiguator for the optional trailing material column** (GiD writes no separator, so a row is `1+Nnode` or `1+Nnode+1` tokens and nothing else); **the `Result` header carries no `:N` dimension suffix**, so `Vector` does not say whether rows hold 2, 3 or 4 components and the count must come from the row width, cross-checked against gidpost's own legal-dims table; and **a `Values` row's id is SUPPRESSED when it repeats the previous row's**, so a G>1 Gauss-point result writes the id once and the following rows begin with whitespace — a row narrower than `1+ncomp` is a continuation, not a new id. Two **real-world variants our own writer structurally cannot produce** shape the data model, both found in a genuine Kratos-produced file rather than reasoned about: the full node table may be **repeated in every `MESH` block** (so node ids accumulate into one global map de-duplicated by id, making a repeated-but-identical table a no-op), and element ids may **restart at 1 per block** (so they are tracked per block, and results resolve through the Gauss-point set's mesh name rather than a global id map). A trailing material column reads back as `cell_data["gmsh:physical"]`, the exact inverse of the write key — but an **all-zero** column is deliberately not surfaced, because the binary and HDF5 writers always emit one and materializing it would invent data and make the three flavours disagree on a round trip. **Arbitrary Gauss points per element are supported** (v10.19.0; previously G>1 was dropped with a warning, since meshio++'s `cell_data` has no per-node-within-cell axis -- MED's ELNO/ELGA limit). A G-point, k-component array is stored **flat** as `(ncells, G*k)`, Gauss-point-major (the order GiD's own `Values` rows arrive in, so neither direction re-packs), declared by `field_data["gid:gauss_points:<name>"]` -- the `gid:result_type:` mechanism reused, including its "declare only what carries information" rule: **`G == 1` declares nothing**, so the historical one-value-per-element path is untouched and byte-identical (pinned by a captured-baseline test). The declaration is required rather than inferred because `(ncells, 3)` is genuinely ambiguous (a 3-vector at one point, or a scalar at three), and the result type's legal widths are checked against **k**, never `G*k`. `Natural Coordinates: Internal` is only legal for specific counts per family (triangle 1/3/6, quad 1/4/9, tetra 1/4/10, hexahedron 1/8/27, wedge 1/6, pyramid 1/5; **line accepts any count and forbids `Given` outright**), so any other G must supply natural coordinates via `field_data["gid:gauss_coords:<celltype>:<G>"]` (`G*dim` doubles, point-major, keyed by `(cell type, G)` because that is what a GiD Gauss set actually depends on) -- omitting them is a `WriteError` naming the type and its legal counts, never a file GiD would reject. Reading captures a `Given` set's coordinates into the same key so it can be written back, **ASCII-only** (binary stores them as raw double records the string-record scanner cannot reach; the HDF5 attribute layout is unverified) -- the *count* is read in all three flavours, so G>1 values round-trip everywhere. **Wiring this surfaced a genuine pre-existing binary-reader bug**: gidpost's `CPostBinary_WriteValues` suppresses a repeated id exactly as the ASCII writer does (its own source comments that this is "only useful when writing values for gauss points in elements"), but the binary reader assumed one id per record, so it misread a value as an id the moment G>1 -- fixed by consuming exactly `G*k` reals after each id, which is sound because `m_LastID` is reset per `Values` block and element ids are unique within one. Separately and **also pre-existing**: the binary stream carries neither a row width nor a component count, so its reader can only use the declared type's canonical width (Scalar 1, Vector 3, Matrix 6) -- a 2-component `Vector` is legal GiD, round-trips through ASCII, and is unrecoverable from binary at *any* G including 1 (pinned by `test_binary_cannot_recover_a_non_canonical_component_count` so it is not mistaken for a Gauss-point defect). **`ResultGroup` is read in the ascii flavour** (v10.19.0): each `ResultDescription` member is unpacked into an ORDINARY result and appended to the same vector a plain `Result` feeds, so point/cell routing, the flat Gauss-point layout, result-type recording and `mTimeStep` all apply unchanged with no second apply path to drift. Member widths come from `:N` when present (a `ResultDescription` is the one place GiD states a width -- a plain `Result` header does not, which is why THAT path infers from the row); absent it the manual fixes them inside a group (`Vector` always 3, `Matrix` always 6), and a type admitting several widths with no `:N` (the `Complex*` family) is refused by name rather than guessed, since picking one silently mis-associates every column after it. **Binary/HDF5 refuse it by name** -- the binary path previously *skipped* the record silently, losing the whole group with no diagnostic, which is fixed. **A related pre-existing defect was fixed alongside**: the optional-`.post.res` handler caught EVERY `ReadError` from the results parse and cleared all results, so the reader's own refusals were unreachable from `read_gid` and a malformed file silently yielded geometry-only -- discarding even results that had parsed cleanly. An absent sibling is still silent (it is optional), but a malformed one now warns naming the problem; note the warning goes to the C++ stderr **file descriptor**, so Python tests need `capfd`, not `capsys`. **`GidMode::AsciiZipped`** (v10.19.0) is the write counterpart to gidpost's `GiD_PostAsciiZipped` -- the same two sibling files, gzipped, sharing the `Ascii` branch entirely since only compression differs; reading already worked via the reader's gzip sniffing. Spelled `"ascii_zipped"` with an underscore, not a hyphen, so no binding needs the symbol translation `gradient`'s hyphenated method names required in Julia; **`Auto` deliberately never resolves to it** (no extension can express "zipped", and inferring would change what every existing `.post.msh` write produces). Appending the enumerator is Tier C additive -- `GidMode` already declares an explicit `: int` underlying type -- so ABI stays 11. **Multi-step series round-trip in both directions** (v10.19.0). Steps live in the `.post.res` headers; `read_gid_metadata` now finds them through a **header-only scan** that skips every `Values` body without tokenising it (metadata must stay cheap), fills `mTimeValues`, and `gid` joins `seq_format_may_have_steps` -- one line that makes `sequence_num_steps`, fan-out, `info --fast` and `TimeSeries` all start working, since they are registry-derived. **That was an unlisted gap, not a roadmap item**: the reader had always honoured `mTimeStep`, but metadata never opened the results sibling, so it reported ONE step whatever the file held and `gid` was invisible to the entire sequence layer -- readable at step N but unable to say how many steps existed. **Both `_sequence.py` twins need updating too** (`_TIME_CAPABLE_READERS`, `_SERIES_WRITERS`): Python's sequence engine is a separate pure-Python implementation, not a shim, so a C++-only change leaves it seeing one step. **`Group`/`OnGroup` is NOT a region concept** and the roadmap bullet proposing that mapping was a category error, now corrected AND implemented for what it really is: a `Group` wraps the `MESH` blocks of ONE mesh serving a CONSECUTIVE RUN of steps, so GiD can swap meshes as the step changes (re-meshing/adaptive analyses); the postprocess format has no node/element set concept at all -- the material column is its only grouping. Blocks and results are TAGGED with their group and the requested step resolves to the group owning it, using only that group's meshes, results and **nodes** (each group carries its own coordinates, so a re-meshed step must not come back carrying every other step's nodes as orphans). Previously the wrapper was skipped and every group's blocks merged into one garbled mesh while `OnGroup` results were dropped outright -- a latent bug, since no test had a multi-group file. **Fan-in is deliberately NOT a public writer class**: `write_gid_series` is a free function that PULLS steps (`rNext` returns false when exhausted -- pull, not push, so a Python generator drives it as naturally as the counted C++ loop), driven by `sequence_to_timeseries`; the transient surface meshio++ exposes IS the sequence layer, which already carries every binding, so no `GidTimeSeriesWriter` with its own C/Fortran/Julia/R/WASM surface was needed -- only one pybind function, because `_sequence.py` cannot reach the C++ branch. A `Group` is emitted only when a step's mesh DIFFERS from the previous one's, so a fixed-mesh series emits none and keeps the single-step output shape, and processing steps in order satisfies GiD's "only one group at a time" rule by construction. `sequence_write_supports_time` must be updated in lockstep -- a gtest cross-checks it against what `sequence_to_timeseries` actually accepts and turns CI red naming itself otherwise. Tensor and complex result types round-trip through the `field_data` declaration mechanism above (see the Matrix/Complex paragraph), values stored verbatim in GiD's own order since meshio++ has no canonical complex/tensor layout of its own to convert to. `ReadOptions::mTimeStep` is honoured natively (a step never decoded cannot be recovered by a caller-side filter) and `gid` is registered in `registry_readers_ex()`/`registry_metadata_readers()` accordingly. **The `GidOrdering` bytes oracle in `tests/cpp/test_gid.cpp` is deliberately KEPT now that a reader exists** — a reader+writer round trip is a weak oracle that a consistently-wrong permutation survives, so it cannot replace a raw-bytes geometric check. Finally, the reader's own test suite surfaced **two genuine upstream gidpost bugs** that made its HDF5 flavour single-use per process: `G_num_HDF5_files` was incremented on open but never decremented on close (so the second HDF5 file in a process was refused), and that refusal path `return NULL`ed **without unlocking** gidpost's process-global mutex, deadlocking every later call — including ones for the unrelated ascii and binary flavours. Both are fixed in the vendored copy, marked inline with `meshio++ fix` comments and recorded in `src/cpp/third_party/gidpost/README.meshioplusplus.md` for re-application on any version bump; a single-file program never reaches either bug, which is why v10.18.0 shipped without noticing.
